<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
\label{def: random ensemble}
    Let $\Sigma$ be a (possibly infinite) interval of $\ensuremath{\mathbb{Z}}$, and let $\Lambda$ be an interval of $\ensuremath{\mathbb{R}}$. Consider the set \(X:=\mathcal{C}^\Sigma\) of continuous functions $f:\Sigma\times \Lambda \rightarrow \ensuremath{\mathbb{R}}$. We endow it with the topology of uniform convergence on compact subsets of $\Sigma\times\Lambda$. Let $\mathcal{C}$ denote the sigma-field  generated by Borel sets in $X$.

    A {\it $\Sigma$-indexed line ensemble} $L$ is a random variable defined on a probability space $(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\ensuremath{\mathbb{P}})$, taking values in $X$ such that $L$ is a $(\mathfrak{B},\mathcal{C})$-measurable function. Furthermore, we write $L_i:=(L(\omega))(i,\cdot)$ for the line indexed by $i\in\Sigma$.
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
\label{def: disjointness}
    Fix $x_1\le x_2$, $x'_1\le x'_2$ and $\ell \ge m$. Then, we say the paths $\pi_1$ and $\pi_2$ from $(x_1, \ell)$ to $(x_2, m)$ and $(x'_1, \ell)$ to $(x'_2, m)$, respectively are
    \begin{itemize}
        \item \emph{essentially disjoint} if the intersection of zigzags $\mathsf{ZigZag}(\pi_1)$ and $\mathsf{ZigZag}(\pi_2)$ contains no horizontal line segment of positive length,
        \item \emph{disjoint} if the zigzags $\mathsf{ZigZag}(\pi_1)$ and $\mathsf{ZigZag}(\pi_2)$ have empty intersection.
    \end{itemize}
    Note that being disjoint implies being essentially disjoint, as the latter is equivalent to the zig-zags $\mathsf{ZigZag}(\pi_1)$ and $\mathsf{ZigZag}(\pi_2)$ sharing no interior of any of their horizontal segments.
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
\label{def: length} Let \(x\le y \in \mathbb{R}\) and \(m < k\in\mathbb{Z}\). For each \(m\le i \le k-1\), let \(t_{k-i}\) denote the jump of the path \(\pi\), on an ensemble \((f_i)_{i\in I}\), from \(f_{i+1}\) to \(f_{i}\). Then the length of \(\pi\) is defined as
\begin{equation}\label{eq: last passage}
\ell(\pi) = f_m(y)-f_m(t_{k-m}) + \displaystyle\sum_{i = 1}^{k-m-1}(f_{k-i}(t_{i+1})-f_{k-i}(t_{i}))+f_{k}(t_{1})-f_{k}(x).
\end{equation}
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{lemma: Metric Composition}
    Let \(x\le y \in \mathbb{R}\), \(m < \ell\in\mathbb{Z}\) and \(f\in \mathcal{C}^I\). If \(k\in \{m, \dots, \ell\}\), then we have
    \[
    f[(x,\ell)\to(y,m)] = \displaystyle \sup_{z\in[x,y]}(f[(x,\ell)\to(z,k)]+f[(z,k)\to(y,m)])\,,
    \]
    and if \(k\in \{m+1, \dots, \ell\}\), then 
    \[
    f[(x,\ell)\to(y,m)] = \displaystyle \sup_{z\in[x,y]}(f[(x,\ell)\to(z,k)]+f[(z,k-1)\to(y,m)]).
    \]
    Furthermore for any \(z\in [x,y]\), 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq: composition}
    f[(x,\ell)\to(y,m)] = \displaystyle \sup_{k\in \{m, \dots, \ell\}}(f[(x,\ell)\to(z,k)]+f[(z,k)\to(y,m)])
    \end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{lemma: geod location brown env lpp}
    Fix $2\le \ell \le n$, $a< b\in \ensuremath{\mathbb{R}}$ and some $f\in\mathcal{C}^n([a, b])$. Then for a last passage geodesic $\pi$ from $(a, n)$ to $(b, 1)$, the jump times $Z_\ell(a, b)$ from level $\ell$ to level $\ell-1$ satisfy 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq: geod jump argmax brownian abs env lpp}
        Z_\ell(a, b) \in \operatorname*{argmax}_{s\in [a, b]}(f[(a, n)\to (s, \ell)]+f[(s, \ell-1)\to (b,1)])\,.
    \end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
For fixed $a< b$, if the environment $f\in\mathcal{C}^n[a, b]$ is random and $f-f(a)$ is absolutely continuous with respect to $n$ independent Brownian motions on $[a, b]$, then by \cite[Lemma B.1]{hammond2019patchwork} (also Lemmas \cite[2.15, 2.16]{dauvergne2022disjointoptimizersdirectedlandscape}), there is a unique geodesic almost surely, that is the left-most and right-most geodesics coincide almost surely. For instance, the above is true for the Airy line ensemble and Dyson Brownian motion (for Dyson Brownian motion, this claim is only true for intervals away from the origin, that is $a> 0$), discussed in Sections \ref{subsec: melons} and \ref{subsec: Airy line ensemble}. Note Brownian $n$-melons and $n$-level Dyson Brownian motion are equal in law for all $n\ge 1$. For Dyson Brownian motion, this is essentially due to the Brownian Gibbs property, see Definition 2.2 in \cite{corwin2014brownian} and Lemmas 2.15-2.16 in \cite{dauvergne2022disjointoptimizersdirectedlandscape}. 
    
    Under this supposed local absolute continuity with respect to Brownian motion, one obtains almost surely for all rational $q< r$ in $[a, b]$ and $m \le k$, the existence of unique geodesics in $f$ with endpoints $(q, k)$ and $(r, m)$. Henceforth, we will tacitly assume in such environments, the existence of unique geodesics starting and ending at countably many endpoints.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lemma: geod tree}
    Let $f:\ensuremath{\mathbb{N}}\times\ensuremath{\mathbb{R}}\to\ensuremath{\mathbb{R}}$ be any sequence of random continuous functions such that when restricted to $\{1, \ldots, n\}\times [a, b]$ for $a< b$, $f-f(a)$ is absolutely continuous with respect to independent Brownian motions. Then, for any fixed $x< y< z \in \ensuremath{\mathbb{R}}$ and $m\le n$, almost surely the set of overlap of geodesics in the environment $f$ starting from $(x, n), (y,n)$ and ending at $(z, m)$
    \begin{align*}
        I:= \bigg\{ s\in [y, z]: [&\pi[(x, n)\to(z,m)](s), \lim_{r\to s^-}\pi[(x, n)\to(z,m)](r)]\\
        \cap  [&\pi[(y, n)\to(z,m)](s), \lim_{r\to s^-}\pi[(y, n)\to(z,m)](r)]\neq \emptyset\bigg\}
    \end{align*}
    is a non-empty interval (with a possibly empty interior).
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
Since the paths $\pi_1: = \pi[(x, n)\to(z,m)]$, $\pi_2:= \pi[(y, n)\to(z,m)]$ have the same endpoint, the interval $I$ contains $y$, and is not empty. Now, suppose $s< t$ both in $[x, y]$ are also in $I$. Then, we show that all $u\in (s, t)$ belong to $I$ also. Indeed, suppose for a contradiction $u$ was not in $I$, then the restrictions of the zig-zags of $\pi_1, \pi_2$ to $[s, t]$ would not coincide. This would result in a `geodesic bubble' (see Figure \ref{fig: geod tree}, and so by the restriction property of geodesics, one would then be able to violate geodesic uniqueness in $f$, by switching between the two (red and black) line segments forming the `geodesic bubble'.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
This lemma also gives as a result the \emph{geodesic monotonicity} property on a locally Brownian environment $f$ as in the statement therein. This means for any endpoints $(x, n)$, $(y, n)$ as in the above statement, the zig-zags of the geodesic starting from $x$ is to the left and below (above in the figure due to the reverse ordering) that of $y$. One can obtain an entirely analogous ordering of geodesics when considering distinct endpoints $(z, m)$ and $(w, m)$, $z < w$.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{lemma: crossing}
Let $f:\ensuremath{\mathbb{N}}\times\ensuremath{\mathbb{R}}\to\ensuremath{\mathbb{R}}$ be any sequence of continuous functions. Let $m_1\le m_2\le n_1\le n_2$, and $y_1\le y_2\le x_1\le x_2$. Then we have
\[
f[(y_1,n_1)\to(x_1,m_1)] + f[(y_2,n_2)\to(x_2,m_2)] \ge
f[(y_1,n_1)\to(x_2,m_2)] + f[(y_2,n_2)\to(x_1,m_1)].
\]
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lemma: crossing equiavlence}
        Let $f:\ensuremath{\mathbb{N}}\times\ensuremath{\mathbb{R}}\to\ensuremath{\mathbb{R}}$ be any sequence of random continuous functions such that when restricted to $\{1, \ldots, n\}\times [a, b]$ for $a< b$, $f-f(a)$ is absolutely continuous with respect to independent Brownian motions. Then, with $1\le m_1\le  m_2\le n_1\le n_2$ and $y_1\le y_2\le x_1\le x_2$ as in Lemma \ref{lemma: crossing}, the event
    \[
    \pi[(y_1,n_1)\to(x_1,m_1)] \; \text{and}\; \pi[(y_2,n_2)\to(x_2,m_2)]\;\text{are disjoint},
    \]
    is equivalent (up to null sets) to the event
    \[
    f[(y_1,n_1)\to(x_1,m_1)] + f[(y_2,n_2)\to(x_2,m_2)] >
    f[(y_1,n_1)\to(x_2,m_2)] + f[(y_2,n_2)\to(x_1,m_1)]
    \]
    where $\pi[\cdot \to \cdot]$ denote the almost sure unique geodesics in the environment $f$. Here, we emphasise disjointness is in terms of the \emph{zig-zags} of the paths in question.
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
We prove the forward implication. Observe that the metric composition law, Lemma \ref{lemma: Metric Composition} implies that for any $a< b$, $m\le n$ and geodesic $\pi$ from $(a, n)$ to $(b, m)$ in the environment $f$, (recall Definition \ref{def: length})
    \begin{align}\label{eq: last passage zigzag}
        &\ell(\pi|_{[a, z]}) = f[(a, n)\to (z, \pi(z))]\; \text{and}\nonumber\\
        &\ell(\pi|_{[z, b]}) = f[(z, j)\to (b, m)]\;\text{for all}\; j\in \{\pi(z), \ldots, \lim_{t\to z^-}\pi(t)\}.
    \end{align}

    Now, by planarity, there exists some $(z, \ell)$ that lies on the intersection of the induced zigzags (recall their definition in \eqref{eq: zig-zag}) of $\pi[(y_1,n_1)\to(x_2,m_2)]$ and $\pi[(y_2,n_2)\to(x_1,m_1)]$. 
    
    On the event 
    \[
    \pi[(y_1,n_1)\to(x_1,m_1)] \; \text{and}\; \pi[(y_2,n_2)\to(x_2,m_2)]\;\text{are disjoint},
    \]
    $(z, \ell)$ must not be in the zig-zag of at least one of $\pi[(y_1,n_1)\to(x_1,m_1)]$ and $\pi[(y_2,n_2)\to(x_2,m_2)]$. Since geodesics are unique, we can without loss of generality, suppose that $(z, \ell)$ does not lie on $\pi[(y_1,n_1)\to(x_1,m_1)]$. This in conjunction with \eqref{eq: last passage zigzag} gives the inequalities
    \[
    f[(y_1,n_1)\to(x_1,m_1)] > f[(y_1,n_1)\to(z,\ell)] + f[(z,\ell)\to(x_1,m_1)]
    \]
    and
    \[
    f[(y_2,n_2)\to(x_2,m_2)] \ge  f[(y_2,n_2)\to(z,\ell)] + f[(z,\ell)\to(x_2,m_2)]\,.
    \]
    Adding the above inequalities gives the conclusion by observing (using \eqref{eq: last passage}) that 
    \begin{align*}
        f[(y_1,n_1)\to(z,\ell)] + &f[(z,\ell)\to(x_1,m_1)] + f[(y_2,n_2)\to(z,\ell)] + f[(z,\ell)\to(x_2,m_2)]\\
        = &f[(y_1,n_1)\to(x_2,m_2)] + f[(y_2,n_2)\to(x_1,m_1)].
    \end{align*}

    The reverse implication is proved in an entirely analogous manner. In fact, this is a deterministic result and can be established by considering the contrapositive of this implication, by a switching argument (see the proof of Lemma \ref{lemma: geod tree}).
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{lemma: Pitman melon}
    Let \(f\in \mathcal{C}^2([0, \infty))\) and let \(Wf = (Wf_1, Wf_2)\) be as above. Then for all \(t\in[0,\infty)\),
    \[
    Wf_1(t) = \displaystyle \max_{i=1,2}\{f_i(0) + f [(0, i) \to (t, 1)]\} .
    \]
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm: melon scaling Airy}
    Let \(WB^n\)  be a Brownian \(n\)-melon constructed from a family of independent standard (rate one) Brownian motions $B_1, \ldots, B_n$.  Define the rescaled melon \(A^n = (A^n_1, \dots, A^n_n)\) for $1\le i \le n$ and $y\ge -n^{1/3}/2$ by
\[
A^n_i(y) = n^{1/6} \left((WB^n)_i(1 + 2yn^{-1/3}) - 2\sqrt{n} - 2yn^{1/6} \right).
\]
Then \(A^n\) converges to a random sequence of functions \(\mathcal{A} = (\mathcal{A}_1, \mathcal{A}_2, \dots) \in \mathcal{C}^\mathbb{N}\) in law with respect to product of uniform-on-compact topology on \(\mathcal{C}^\mathbb{N}\). Almost surely, for all \(y \in \mathbb{R}\) and \(i < j\), we have \(\mathcal{A}_i(y) > \mathcal{A}_j(y)\). The function \(\mathcal{A}\) is called the \textbf{(parabolic) Airy line ensemble.}
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{lemma: airy counting 1}
Fix $\ell \ge 1$. Then there exist universal constants $c, C>0$ such that for all $a> 0$, 
    \begin{align*}
 \ensuremath{\mathbb{P}}\bigl(\mathcal{A}_\ell(0)\le-a\bigr)\le C\exp\bigl(\ell-ca^{3/2}\bigr)\,.
\end{align*}
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
Let $N_a=\#\{i:\mathcal{A}_i(0)\ge-a\}$ count the number of points in the Airy line ensemble at zero that exceed the threshold $-a$. By
\cite[Lemma~5.1(i)]{Dauvergne_2021}, the expectation of $N_a$ satisfies the asymptotics
\[
 \mathbb{E} N_a=\frac{2}{3\pi}a^{3/2}+O(1)
 \qquad a\to\infty\,.
\]
Moreover, $N_a$ has the distribution of a sum of independent Bernoulli
random variables, as recalled in the proof of
\cite[Lemma~5.2]{Dauvergne_2021}. Denoting their success probabilities by $(p_j)_{j\ge 1}$,
then
\[
 \mathbb{E} \mathrm{e}^{-N_a}
 =\prod^\infty_{j=1}\bigl(1-(1-\mathrm{e}^{-1})p_j\bigr)
 \le \exp\bigl(-(1-\mathrm{e}^{-1})\mathbb{E} N_a\bigr).
\]
Markov's inequality applied to $\mathrm{e}^{-N_a}$ gives
\begin{align*}
 \ensuremath{\mathbb{P}}\bigl(\mathcal{A}_\ell(0)\le-a\bigr)
 &\le\ensuremath{\mathbb{P}}(N_a\le\ell)\le e^\ell\mathbb{E} \mathrm{e}^{-N_a}\le C\exp\bigl(\ell-ca^{3/2}\bigr)\,, \qquad a\to \infty
\end{align*}
for some universal constants $c, C> 0$. Enlarging $C$ extends the above bound to all $a> 0$.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{lemma: airy counting 2}
For every sufficiently small $a>0$, there exist constants
$c_a,C_a>0$ such that
\[
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{P}}\bigl(\mathcal{A}_k(0)>-4a k^{2/3}\bigr)
    \le C_a\exp\bigl(-c_a k\log(k+1)\bigr),
    \qquad k\ge1.
\]
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
As in Lemma \ref{lemma: airy counting 1}, set $  N_R:=\#\{i\ge1:\mathcal{A}_i(0)\ge-R\}$.
The Airy counting estimates in \cite[Lemma~5.1]{Dauvergne_2021} give
\[
    \mathbb{E} N_R=(R/\kappa)^{3/2}+O(1),
    \qquad
    \operatorname{Var}(N_R)=O(\log R),
    \qquad R\to\infty\,.
\]
where $\kappa = (3\pi/2)^{2/3}$. Moreover, recall from Lemma \ref{lemma: airy counting 1}, $N_R$ has the law of a sum of independent Bernoulli
random variables. Choose $a>0$ so small that $(4a/\kappa)^{3/2}<\frac12$ and set $R_k=4a k^{2/3}$. For all sufficiently large $k$, we estimate the mean and variance of of $N_{R_k}$
\[
    \mu_k:=\mathbb{E} N_{R_k}\le k/2,
    \qquad
    v_k:=\operatorname{Var}(N_{R_k})\le C_a\log k\,,
\]
for some constant $C_a> 0$.

Now, for a Bernoulli random variable $X$ with success probability $p$, we can estimate the moment generating function of the centred random variable $Y:= X-p$, obtaining by a Taylor expansion using $|Y|\le 1$, $\mathbb{E}Y = 0$ and $\mathbb{E}Y^2 = p(1-p)$,
\[
\mathbb{E}\mathrm{e}^{\theta Y} \le 1 + p(1-p)(\mathrm{e}^\theta - 1- \theta)\,,\qquad \theta \ge 0\,.
\]
By the above Bernoulli representation for $N_R$, the above bound gives
\[
    \log\mathbb{E} \mathrm{e}^{\theta(N_{R_k}-\mu_k)}
    \le v_k(e^\theta-1-\theta),
    \qquad \theta\ge 0\,.
\]
The Markov inequality applied to $\mathrm{e}^{\theta(N_{R_k}-\mu_k)}$ for a suitable choice of $\theta \ge 0$ gives
\[
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{P}}(N_{R_k}-\mu_k\ge b)
    \le
    \exp\left[-v_k h\left(\frac{b}{v_k}\right)\right]\,, \quad b \ge 0\,, \qquad h(r):=(1+r)\log(1+r)-r\,.
\]
For $b=k-\mu_k\ge k/2$ and $v_k\le C_a\log k$, the exponent is
at most $-c_a k\log k$, for some constant $c_a> 0$. Indeed, this follows from the bounds
\[
\begin{aligned}
v_k h(b/v_k)
&= (v_k+b)\log(1+b/v_k)-b\\
&\ge b\bigl[\log(1+b/v_k)-1\bigr]\\
&\ge \frac{k}{2}
\left[
\log\!\left(1+\frac{k}{2C_a\log k}\right)-1
\right]\\
&\ge c_a k\log k,
\end{aligned}
\]
for all sufficiently large \(k\). Hence we arrive at the tail bounds,
\[
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{P}}(N_{R_k}\ge k)\le C_a \mathrm{e}^{-c_a k\log k}\,,
\]
for all $k$ large enough. Since the inclusion $\{\mathcal{A}_k(0)>-R_k\}\subseteq\{N_{R_k}\ge k\}$ holds for any $k\ge 1$, the result follows after adjusting the constants to accommodate the remaining finitely many values of $k$.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label{lemma: finite-depth-tail}
Fix $k\ge 1$ and $T>0$. Then, there exist universal constants $c,C>0$ such that
\[
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{P}}\bigl(\mathcal{A}[(0, k)\to (T, 1)]+T^2>u\bigr)
    \le \exp\left(Ck-\frac{cu^2}{T}\right)
\]
for every $k\ge1$, $T>0$, and $u>0$.

In particular, for every fixed $a,T>0$, there exist constants
$c_{a,T},C_{a,T}>0$ such that
\[
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{P}}\bigl(\mathcal{A}[(0, k)\to (T, 1)]>a k^{2/3}\bigr)
    \le C_{a,T}\exp(-c_{a,T}k^{4/3}).
\]
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
Fix $k\ge 1, T> 0$ and $u> 0$.

Fix a finite mesh $D\subset[0,T]$ containing $0$ and $T$. Moreover, for a continuous environment $f=(f_1,\ldots,f_k)$, define the `coarse-grained' last passage values with respect to the mesh $D$,
\[
    \Lambda_{k, D}(f;T)
    :=
    \sup_{\substack{0=t_k\le t_{k-1}\le\cdots\le t_0=T\\t_i \in D}}
    \sum_{i=1}^k
       \bigl(f_i(t_{i-1})-f_i(t_i)\bigr).
\]
Since the environment is continuous, we have the monotone convergence of $\Lambda_{k, D_n}(f;T)$ to the actual last passage value $f[(0, k) \to (T, 1)]$ for a family $(D_n)_n$ of nested meshes with mesh size tending to zero as $n\to \infty$. For every $\lambda>0$, $n\ge 1$ the functional
\[
    f\longmapsto
    \exp\bigl(\lambda\Lambda_{k,D_n}(f;T)\bigr)
\]
is convex and depends on finitely many evaluations of $f$.
Indeed, $\Lambda_{k,D_n}$ is a finite maximum of linear
functionals, and the exponential is convex and increasing. 

Fix $\lambda > 0$ and some mesh $D$ of $[0, T]$. Let $\underline{x} = (x_1, \ldots, x_n)$ be such that $x_1 > x_2 > \ldots > x_n$. Let $\Gamma^{n, \underline{x}}: =(\Gamma^{n, \underline{x}}_1, \ldots \Gamma^{n, \underline{x}}_n)$ by a $n$-level rate one Dyson Brownian motion starting from $\underline{x}$. Since its law on $\{1, \ldots, n\}\times D$ is a log-concave perturbation of independent Brownian motions (see Lemma 3.2 in \cite{wu2025applicationsoptimaltransportdyson}), an application of the contraction result in Proposition 2.9 of \cite{wu2025applicationsoptimaltransportdyson} gives the estimate
\[
    \mathbb{E}\exp\bigl(\lambda\Lambda_{k,D}(\Gamma^{n, \underline{x}} - \mathbb{E}[\Gamma^{n, \underline{x}}];T)\bigr)
    \le
    \mathbb{E}\exp\bigl(\lambda\Lambda_{k,D}(B;T)\bigr),
\]
where $B:= (B_1, \ldots, B_n)$ is an independent family of standard Brownian motions. By \cite[Proposition 4.3.5]{anderson2010introduction}, as $\underline {x}\to \underline{0}$ pointwise, the laws of $\Gamma^{\underline{x}}$ converge in path space $\mathcal{C}^n(\ensuremath{\mathbb{R}}_{\ge 0})$ to those of $n$-level Dyson Brownian motion starting from the origin (recall it has the same law as the $n$-melon $WB^n$ from Subsection \ref{subsec: melons}). Also, using Lemma 3.2 in \cite{konig2001eigenvalues}, it is not hard to establish the convergence of expectations $\mathbb{E}[\Gamma^{n, \underline{x}}(t)]$ to $\mathbb{E}[\Gamma^{n, \underline{0}}(t)] = \mathbb{E}[WB^n(t)]$ for all $t\ge 0$. Thus, by Fatou's lemma, taking $\underline{x}\to 0$, we have 
\[
    \mathbb{E}\exp\bigl(\lambda\Lambda_{k,D}(WB^n - \mathbb{E}[WB^n];T)\bigr)
    \le
    \mathbb{E}\exp\bigl(\lambda\Lambda_{k,D}(B;T)\bigr)\,.
\]
Now, in accordance with the edge scaling in Theorem \ref{thm: melon scaling Airy}, the above estimate in conjunction with the invariance of Brownian motion under diffusive scaling gives
\[
    \mathbb{E}\exp\bigl(\lambda\Lambda_{k,D}(A^n - \mathbb{E}[A^n];T)\bigr)
    \le
    \mathbb{E}\exp\bigl(\lambda\Lambda_{k,D}(\sqrt{2}B;T)\bigr)\,,
\]
where
\[
A^n_i(y) = n^{1/6} \left((WB^n)_i(1 + 2yn^{-1/3}) - 2\sqrt{n} - 2yn^{1/6} \right)\,,\quad 1\le i \le n\,.
\]
By Skorokhod's representation theorem, we can take the $A^n_i$ to converge locally uniformly to some coupled version of the Airy line ensemble $\mathcal{A}$. Moreover, by
Lemmas 2.2-2.3 \cite{gustavsson2005gaussian} and \cite{ledoux2010small} (for details, see the proof of Lemma 5.7 in \cite{dauvergne2026downthemiddle}), the expectations $\mathbb{E}[A^n_i(y)]\to \mathbb{E}[\mathcal{A}_i(y)]$ as $n\to \infty$ for all $i\ge 1$, $y\in \ensuremath{\mathbb{R}}$. Thus, another application of Fatou's lemma gives the estimate (recalling that the last passage values of an environment where each level is shifted by the same function are related to the original last passage values simply by the addition of the increment of the perturbing function from the initial to the terminal spatial values)
\[
     \mathbb{E}\exp\bigl(\lambda\Lambda_{k,D}(\mathcal{R};T)\bigr) = \mathbb{E}\exp\bigl(\lambda\Lambda_{k,D}(\mathcal{A}-\mathbb E\mathcal{A};T)\bigr)
    \le
    \mathbb{E}\exp\bigl(\lambda\Lambda_{k,D}(\sqrt{2} B;T)\bigr)\,,
\]
with $\mathcal{R}(\cdot) = \mathcal{A}(\cdot) + (\cdot)^2$ the stationary Airy line ensemble. 

Taking nested meshes $D_n$ with dyadic times, continuity and monotone convergence yield
\[
    \mathbb{E}\exp\bigl(\lambda\mathcal{A}[(0, k) \to (T, 1)]+\lambda T^2\bigr)=\mathbb{E}\exp\bigl(\lambda\mathcal{R}[(0, k) \to (T, 1)]\bigr)
    \le \mathbb{E}\exp\bigl(\sqrt{2}\lambda B[(0,k)\to(T,1)]\bigr)\,.
\]
By the Brownian last-passage/GUE identity (see \cite{baryshnikov2000random}), the last passage value $B[(0,k)\to(T,1)]$ has the
law of the largest eigenvalue of a suitably normalized
$k\times k$ GUE matrix. Gaussian concentration, \cite[Proposition 3.1]{ledoux2007deviation}, gives
\[
\ensuremath{\mathbb{P}}\big(B[(0,k)\to(1,1)] \ge r + \mathbb{E}B[(0,k)\to(1,1)]\big)\le 2\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{r^2}{2}}\,,\quad r\ge 0\,.
\]
Since by equation (3.4) in \cite[p. 194]{ledoux2007deviation}, $\mathbb{E}B[(0,k)\to(T,1)] \le 2\sqrt{2kT}$, we estimate by Fubini's theorem and the invariance of Brownian motion under diffusive rescaling,
\[
\mathbb{E}\exp\bigl(\sqrt{2}\lambda B[(0,k)\to(T,1)]\bigr)
\le C\exp\bigl(A\lambda\sqrt{kT}+D\lambda^2T\bigr)\,,\qquad \lambda > 0\,,
\]
for absolute constants $A,C, D > 0$. An application of Markov's inequality gives for any $\lambda > 0$
\begin{align}\label{eq: moment gen func airy lpp}
     \ensuremath{\mathbb{P}}\bigl(\mathcal{A}[(0, k)\to (T, 1)]+T^2>u\bigr)
    &\le \frac{\mathbb{E}\exp\bigl(\lambda\mathcal{A}[(0, k) \to (T, 1)]+\lambda T^2\bigr)}{\exp \lambda u}\\
    &\le  \frac{\mathbb{E}\exp\bigl(\sqrt{2}\lambda B[(0,k)\to(T,1)]\bigr)}{\exp \lambda u}\\
    &\le C\mathrm{e}^{A\lambda\sqrt{kT}+ D\lambda^2 T-\lambda u}\qquad u\ge0\,.
\end{align}
Taking $\lambda= (u-A\sqrt{kT})/(2DT)$, we have
\[
\ensuremath{\mathbb{P}}\bigl(\mathcal{A}[(0,k)\to(T,1)]+T^2>u\bigr)
\le C\exp\left(
    -\frac{(u-A\sqrt{kT})^2}{4DT}
\right)\,, \qquad u \ge A\sqrt{kT}\,.
\]
Extending the bound trivially for all $0 \le u \le A\sqrt{kT}$ we obtain
\begin{align*}
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{P}}\bigl(\mathcal{A}[(0, k)\to (T, 1)]+T^2>u\bigr)\le \exp\left(Ck -\frac{cu^2}{T}\right)\qquad u\ge 0\,,
\end{align*}
for some absolute constants $c, C > 0$.

For the final part, simply use $u = T^2 + ak^{\frac{2}{3}}$.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label{lemma: shape}
Fix $0<a<b<\infty$, then there exist some (possibly $a, b$-dependent) constants $c,C>0$ such that for
$T\in[a,b]$, $k\ge3$ and $1\le s\le k^{1/14}$,
\begin{align}\label{eq:shape}
 &\ensuremath{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left|\mathcal{A}[(0,k)\to(T,1)]+T^2
                 -2\sqrt{2kT}\right|>Ck^{3/7}s^{3/2}\right)\notag\\
 &\hspace{25mm}\le Ck^2e^{-cs}+Cke^{-ck^{1/63}}.
\end{align}
<!-- end lemma-10 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
The bridge representation of \cite[Theorem~7.2]{Dauvergne_2021} couples the top $k$ curves of the Airy line ensemble with another bridge ensemble $\mathcal B$ on $[0,T]$, close in total variation distance. Motivated by the Brownian Gibbs property for the Airy line ensemble, $\mathcal{B}$ is defined by first sampling $\mathcal{A}$ on a lattice approximation of $[0, T]\times \{1, \ldots, k\}$ and then for every line interval on the lattice, sampling independent rate two Brownian bridges with endpoints the values of $\mathcal{A}$ on the lattice. Finally, for adjacent endpoints that are sufficiently close together, a non-intersection condition on the Brownian bridges is imposed.

One can precisely construct $\mathcal{B}$ as follows. Fix $0< a< b<\infty$, $k, \ell\ge 3$, $\delta > 0$. With $s_j = Tj/\ell$, $0\le j \le \ell$, sample the points $\mathcal{A}_i(s_j)$, $1\le i \le 2k$, $0\le j \le \ell$. Let $G_{2k}$ be the random sub-graph of $\{1, \ldots, 2k\}\times \{1, \ldots, \ell\}$ where two edges $(i, j)$ and $(i+1, j)$, $1\le i \le 2k-1$, $1\le j \le \ell$ are connected if an only if at least one of $|\mathcal{A}_{i+1}(s_j)-\mathcal{A}_{i}(s_{j})|$, $|\mathcal{A}_{i+1}(s_{j-1})-\mathcal{A}_{i}(s_{j-1})|$ is at most $\delta$. Then for every $(i, j) \in \{1, \ldots, 2k\}\times \{1, \ldots, \ell\}$ sample independent rate two Brownian bridges $B_{i, j}: [s_{j-1}, s_j]\to \ensuremath{\mathbb{R}}$ vanishing at both endpoints. The ensemble $\mathcal{B}$ is obtained by connecting up all of the bridges $B_{i, j}$ by random affine terms $L_{i, j}$ so that for all $(i, j)\in \{1, \ldots, 2k\}\times \{1, \ldots, \ell\}$, $\mathcal{B}_i(s_j) = \mathcal{A}_i(s_j)$ and then imposing the non-intersection condition on the shifted bridges $B_{i, j} + L_{i, j}$ and $B_{i+1, j}+L_{i+1, j}$, for $1\le i \le 2k-1$, $1\le j \le \ell$ if an only if $(i, j)$ and $(i+1, j)$ are in the same connected component of the graph $G_{2k}$.

Theorem~1.1 in \cite{Dauvergne_2021} then gives that with the choice of parameters 
\[
 \gamma>0,\qquad
 \ell=\lceil Tk^{2/3+\gamma}\rceil,\qquad \delta = k^{-1/3-\gamma/4}\,,
\]
for $\gamma> 0$ to be chose later, the ensembles $\mathcal{B}|_{ \{1, \ldots, k \}\times [0, T]}$ and $\mathcal{A}|_{ \{1, \ldots, k \}\times [0, T]}$ are within $Ck^{2/3+\gamma}\mathrm{e}^{-ck^{\gamma/12}}$ in total variation distance, for some absolute constant $c>0$ and some $C>0$ (whence the second term in \eqref{eq:shape} for appropriate $\gamma$). Here we absorbed $T$ into $C$, and taking into account that $a\le T
\le b$, suppressing dependence on $a, b$. There thus exists a coupling between $\mathcal{A}, \mathcal{B}$ such that the first $k$ lines agree on $[0, T]$ with probability at least $1-Ck^{2/3+\gamma}\mathrm{e}^{-ck^{\gamma/12}}$. We will henceforth work with this coupling.

We enlarge the probability space with $N_{i, j}$ independent centred Gaussian with unit variance and independent rate two Brownian bridges $W_{i,j}$, $1\le i \le k$, $1\le j \le \ell$ with domains $[s_{j-1}, s_j]$ vanishing at both endpoints, also jointly independent from the bridge ensemble $\mathcal{B}$ and the $N_{i, j}$. Condition on the values of the Bridge ensemble on the lattice $\{\mathcal{B}_i(s_j) : 0\le j \le \ell, 1\le i \le 2k\}$. Note in particular, the random graph $G_{2k}$ depends on the Airy line ensemble on this lattice. Then, for any isolated vertex $(i, j)$, observe that the Bridge ensemble $\mathcal{B}$ on $[s_{j-1}, s_j]\times\{i\}$ Brownian is a deterministic affine shift of a rate two Brownian bridge vanishing at both endpoints also independent from $\mathcal{B}$ on $[s_{j-1}, s_j]\times\{i'\}$, $i'\neq i$. If on the other hand $(i, j)$ is a non-isolated vertex of $G_{2k}$, one can express the Bridge ensemble $\mathcal{B}$ on $[s_{j-1}, s_j]\times\{i\}$ as the sum of a deterministic affine term depending on the Bridge ensemble lattice values, the Brownian Bridge $W_{i, j}$ and another random continuous $X_{i, j} := B_{i, j} - W_{i, j}$. In this case, the Brownian bridge $B_{i, j}$, vanishing at both endpoints, is conditioned to not intersect $U_{i, j}-1$ other bridges, where $U_{i, j}$ denotes the size of the connected component in $G_{2k}$ containing $(i,j)$. Concatenating the above paths, and undoing the conditioning on the Bridge ensemble lattice values gives the path decomposition for the line $\mathcal{B}$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq: bridge decomp}
    \mathcal B_{i}(s)= H_{i}(s)+R_{i}(s)+X_i(s)\,,\qquad 1\le i\le k\,, 0\le s\le T\,,
\end{equation}
where the $H_i$ are independent rate two Brownian motions on $[0, T]$ constructed from the Brownian bridges and affine shifts of Gaussian $N_{i,j}$, $R_{i}$ is a random continuous, piecewise affine term and $X_{i}=0$ on $[s_{j-1}, s_j]$ for $(i, j)$ isolated in $G_{2k}$ and $X_{i} = B_{i, j}-W_{i,j}$ on $[s_{j-1}, s_j]$ for non-isolated $(i,j)$. 

The uniform modulus of continuity estimates for the Airy line ensemble in \cite[Corollary~1.3]{wu2025applicationsoptimaltransportdyson}, 
gives that exist universal constants $C_1, C_2>0$ such that,
for any $i\ge 1$, $1\le j \le \ell$, $K\geq 0$, 
    \begin{align*}
    \mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{t,s\in [s_{j-1},s_j], t\neq s}  \frac{|\mathcal{A}_i(t)+t^2-\mathcal{A}_i(s)-s^2|}{\sqrt{|t-s| \log (2(s_j-s_{j-1})/|t-s|)}} > K \right)\leq C_1\mathrm{e}^{-C_2K^2}\,.
\end{align*}  
and similarly for the rate two Brownian motions $H_i$.

These estimates, combined with the elementary bounds
\begin{equation*}
\sqrt{|t-s|\log 2h/(t-s)}\le \sqrt{h}\sup_{0< \varepsilon \le 1}\sqrt{\varepsilon\log2/\varepsilon}\le Ch^{1/2}\,,\qquad 0 < t-s\le h
\end{equation*}
where $h = T/\ell$ (taking $k$ sufficiently large, $\ell \ge T$ and so $h\le 1$, which we henceforth assume), in conjunction with a union bound over $i, j$ gives for fixed $s\ge 1$ on an event $\mathrm{Osc}_{s, k}$ of probability at least $1- Ck\ell \mathrm{e}^{-cs}$,
\[
 \max_{1\le i\le k,\,1\le j\le \ell}
 \left\{\mathsf{osc}_{[s_{j-1},s_{j}]}(\mathcal{A}_i),
        \mathsf{osc}_{[s_{j-1},s_{j}]}(H_i)\right\}
\le C\sqrt{hs}+C\max_{j}|s^2_j-s^2_{j-1}| \le C\sqrt{hs}+Ch\,,
\]
where for $f\in \mathcal{C}[a,b]$, we set
\[
\mathsf{osc}_{[a, b]}(f):= \sup_{t,s\in [a,b], t\neq s}|f(t)-f(s)| \,.
\]
On the event where $\mathcal{B}|_{ \{1, \ldots, k \}\times [0, T]}= \mathcal{A}|_{ \{1, \ldots, k \}\times [0, T]}$, the above bounds and the bridge decomposition in \eqref{eq: bridge decomp} imply the $X_i$ and the slopes $R'_i$ of the linear terms $R_i$ satisfy the bounds
\[
 \max_{1\le i\le k,1\le j\le \ell}\mathsf{osc}_{[s_{j-1},s_{j}]}X_i\le C\sqrt{hs}+Ch\,,\qquad \text{ and } \displaystyle \max_{1\le i\le k}\|R_i'\|_{\infty, T}\le C\sqrt{s/h}+C\,.
\]
Here we use $\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\infty, T}$ to denote the uniform norm of a continuous function on $[0, T]$. 

\begin{figure}[hb]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.65\linewidth]{airy_lpp_coarse_graining.png}
    \caption{Illustration of path $\pi$ from $(0, k)$ to $(T, 1)$ superimposed on a `coarse-grained' lattice given by slices $[s_{j-1}, s_j]\times \{i\}$ of width $h$, for $(i, j) \in G_{2k}$. For $1\le s \le k^{1/14}$, on the event $E_{k,s}$, the Airy line ensemble in any box $I_n$, $1\le n \le N_k$ is up to $Cs$ lines, simply independent rate two Brownian motions with random linear terms interpolating between the values of $\mathcal{A}$ on the lattice $\{(s_j, i): 0\le j \le \ell\,, 1\le i \le k\}$. Now, the boxes $I_n$ hit by $\pi$ (shaded in \color{gray} gray\color{black}) are at most $N_k+\ell$, as they themselves can be thought of as a `coarse-grained' version of $\pi$.}
    \label{fig: airy lpp coarse graining}
\end{figure}

Fix $\alpha=1/7$ and $3\gamma/4=1/7$, $1\le s \le k^{1/14}$. Partition $1\le i \le k$ into $N_k$ blocks $(I_{n})_{1\le n \le N_k}$ of $\lfloor k^{1/7}\rfloor$ consecutive indices (except for say some residual block, which can be augmented to a block of size $\lfloor k^{1/7} \rfloor$ using indices from $\{k+1, \ldots, 2k\}$ if necessary). By \cite[Proposition 7.4]{Dauvergne_2021}, for any fixed $1\le j \le \ell$, the size of the set
\[
V_j := \{i: 1\le i \le k\,, (i, j) \text{ is not isolated in } G_{2k}\}
\]
intersected with $I_n$ exceeds $Cs$ with probability at most $Ce^{-cs}$. The restriction $s\le k^{1/14}$ is precisely the parameter restriction in the statement of \cite[Proposition~7.4]{Dauvergne_2021}. A union bound over the $N_k$ such blocks and $1\le j \le \ell$ gives
\[
\displaystyle \ensuremath{\mathbb{P}}\left(\bigcup_{\substack{1\le n \le N_k\\ 1\le j \le \ell}}\{|V_j\cap I_n|\ge Cs\}\right) \le Ck^2\cdot \mathrm{e}^{-cs}\,.
\]

Define events
\begin{align*}
    A_{k} &:= \{\mathcal{B}|_{ \{1, \ldots, k \}\times [0, T]} \neq \mathcal{A}|_{ \{1, \ldots, k \}\times [0, T]}\}\\
    V_{k, s}&:= \left\{\begin{aligned}
        &\text{There exist } 1\le j \le \ell, 1\le n \le N_k \text{ such that the total}\\
        &\text{number of non-isolated points } (i, j)\in G_{2k} \text{ where } i \text{ is in}\\
    &\text{the block } I_n \text{ of }\lfloor k^{1/7}\rfloor \text{ consecutive indices exceeds } Cs
    \end{aligned}\right\}\,.
\end{align*}
We have thus established that with the choice $\gamma = 4/21$,
\[
\ensuremath{\mathbb{P}}(A_k\cup V_{k, s}\cup \mathrm{Osc}^c_{s, k})\le Ck^2\cdot \mathrm{e}^{-cs}+Ck\mathrm{e}^{-ck^{1/63}}\,,\qquad 1\le s \le k^{1/14}\,.
\]

We henceforth suppose we are in the good event $E_{k,s}:= A^c_k\cap V^c_{k, s}\cap \mathrm{Osc}_{s,k}$. Now the zig-zag (recall from Definition \ref{eq: zig-zag}) of a non-increasing path $\pi:[0, T] \to \ensuremath{\mathbb{N}}$ from $(0, k)$ to $(T, 1)$ (recall the definition of last passage percolation, Definition \ref{def: length} in Section \ref{sec: preliminaries}) visits at most $N_k+\ell$ rectangles $I_n\times [s_{j-1}, s_j]$, which with the above choice of $\gamma = 4/21$, is at most $Ck^{6/7}$. For an illustration, see Figure \ref{fig: airy lpp coarse graining}. In each visited rectangle, $X_i$ does not identically vanish on at most $Cs$
line segments, on the event $E_{k, s}$. Thus, on $E_{k, s}$ the lengths $\ell^R(\pi)$, $\ell^X(\pi)$ of $\pi$ in the environments give by the ensembles $R_i$ and $X_i$, respectively satisfy
\[
 |\ell^R(\pi)|\le CT\bigl(\sqrt{s/h}+1\bigr),\qquad
 |\ell^X(\pi)|\le Ck^{6/7}s(\sqrt{hs}+h).
\]
Fix $0 < a < b$. Then for $a\le T\le b$, we have $C_a k^{-6/7} \le h \le  C_b k^{-6/7}$, for some constants $C_a, C_b> 0$. Taking suprema over $\pi$ yields on the event $E_{k, s}$
\[
 \left|\mathcal B[(0,k)\to(T,1)]+T^2-H[(0,k)\to(T,1)]\right|
 \le Ck^{3/7}s^{3/2}\,,
\]
absorbing dependence on $a, b$ into $C$ and the $T^2$ term can be absorbed by enlarging the constant $C$, since $k^{3/7}s^{3/2}\ge 1$, where we again suppress dependence on $a, b$. Finally, the Brownian last-passage tail estimates of Theorem 2.5 in \cite{DOV} give the bounds
\[
\sup_{a\le T\le b}\ensuremath{\mathbb{P}}(|H[(0,k)\to(T,1)]-2\sqrt{2kT}|\ge Ck^{3/7}s^{3/2})\le C\mathrm{e}^{-cs}\,,
\]
again, absorbing $a, b$ dependence into $c, C>0$.

Combining the above two-sided estimates and rarity of $E^c_{k, s}$ gives the bounds \eqref{eq:shape}.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- definition-4: body_md -->
(Airy sheet coupling)\label{coupling: Airy sheet}
The Airy sheet $\mathcal{S}(\cdot, \cdot)$ can be coupled with the (parabolic) Airy line ensemble $\mathcal{A}$ so that $\mathcal{S}(0,\cdot)=\mathcal{A}_1(\cdot)$ and almost surely for all $(x,y,z)\in (\ensuremath{\mathbb{Q}}\cap(0, \infty))\times \ensuremath{\mathbb{Q}}^2$\footnote{The above coupling can alternatively be established for all triplets $(x, y , z)\in K$, where $K$ is any fixed countable dense subset of $(0, \infty)\times \ensuremath{\mathbb{R}}^2$. Moreover, the above limits also exist simultaneously for all $(x, y, z)\in (0, \infty)\times \ensuremath{\mathbb{R}}^2$, see remark 8.1 in \cite{DOV}.}, there exists a random integer $K_{x,y,z}$ such that for all $k\ge K_{x,y,z}$ \begin{equation*}\label{eq: defS}
\mathcal{A}\bigg[\bigg(-\sqrt{\frac{k}{2x}}, k\bigg)\to (z,1)\bigg]-\mathcal{A}\bigg[\bigg(-\sqrt{\frac{k}{2x}}, k\bigg)\to (y,1)\bigg]=\mathcal{S}(x,z)-\mathcal{S}(x,y)\,.
\end{equation*}
<!-- end definition-4 -->
<!-- definition-5: body_md -->
\label{def: semi-inf geo} For any fixed $x > 0$ and $y\in \ensuremath{\mathbb{R}}$, $\ell \ge 1$. A non-increasing cadlag path
\[
\pi[x\to (y, \ell)]: (-\infty, y] \longrightarrow \{\ell, \ell+1,\ldots\}
\]
is called an $x$-directed \emph{semi-infinite geodesic} with endpoint $(y, \ell)$ if all restrictions thereof to bounded intervals are geodesics, and 
\[
\lim_{t\to -\infty}\frac{\pi[x\to (y, \ell)](t)}{t^2} = 2x\,.
\]
<!-- end definition-5 -->
<!-- lemma-11: body_md -->
\label{lemma: semi-inf geod properties}
	\begin{enumerate}
		\item[(1)] For any fixed $x > 0$, $y\in \ensuremath{\mathbb{R}}$, $\ell\ge 1$, almost surely there exists a unique semi-infinite geodesic $\pi[x\to (y, \ell)]$ in $\mathcal{A}$ from $x$ to $(y, \ell)$. 
        \item[(2)] There exists an event $\Omega_0$ of full probability such that on $\Omega_0$, for every $x\ge 0$, $y\in \ensuremath{\mathbb{R}}$, there exist semi-infinite geodesics $\pi^L[x \to (y, \ell)]$ and $\pi^R[x \to (y, \ell)]$ from $x$ to $(y, \ell)$ satisfying $\pi^L[x \to (y, \ell)](t) \le\pi (t) \le\pi^R[x \to (y, \ell)](t)$ for any geodesic $\pi$ from $x$ to $(y, \ell)$ and all $t \in (-\infty, y]$. We call $\pi^L[x \to (y, \ell)]$ and $\pi^R[x \to (y, \ell)]$ the \textbf{leftmost and rightmost geodesics from $x$ to $(y, \ell)$}.
		\item[(3)] (Coalescence) For a fixed $x > 0$ and $y, y' \in \ensuremath{\mathbb{R}}$, $\ell, \ell'\ge 1$ and any geodesics $\pi$ and $\pi'$ from $x$ to $(y, \ell)$ and $x$ to $(y', \ell')$, almost surely we have $\pi(z) = \pi'(z)$ for all sufficiently negative $z$.\\
        \item[(4)] (Shift invariance) For any $x > 0$, $y\in \ensuremath{\mathbb{R}}$ and $\ell \ge 1$, we have the equality in law between
        \[
        \pi[x\to (0, \ell)](\cdot) \text{ and } \pi[x\to (y, \ell)](y+\cdot)\,.
        \]
	\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-11 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
We tacitly restrict ourselves to the event of the existence and uniqueness of geodesics in the Airy line ensemble simultaneously for any countable collection of endpoints, which we may augment as needed. Recall this is guaranteed by the remark after Lemma \ref{lemma: geod location brown env lpp}.

Items $(1), (2), (3)$ with $\ell = \ell' = 1$ are established in Lemma 4.2 of \cite{dauvergne2022disjointoptimizersdirectedlandscape}. Item $(4)$ with $\ell = 1$ is established in Lemma 3.4 of \cite{sarkar2021brownian}. We now focus on $\ell, \ell'\ge 2$. 

Fix $x> 0$, $y\in \ensuremath{\mathbb{R}}$, $\ell \ge 2$. By \cite[Lemma 3.4]{sarkar2021brownian}, one can construct an $x$-directed semi-infinite geodesic starting ending at $(y, 1)$ as the pointwise limit of geodesics $\pi[x_k\to (y, 1)]$ as $k\to \infty$ (recall from Section \ref{sec: semi-inf geod}, $x_k:=(-\sqrt{k}/\sqrt{2x}, k)$, $k\ge 1$). 

We now show that in the same fashion, for any $\ell \ge 2$ the paths $\pi[x_k\to (y, \ell)]$ also lead to consistent object, that is converge pointwise to a limiting path (recall from Definition \ref{def: length}) $\sigma$, satisfying $\lim_{t\to -\infty}\sigma(t)/t^2 = 2x$ whose restriction on any interval is a geodesic. 

By \cite[Lemma 3.4]{sarkar2021brownian}, that is the shift invariance (which incidentally is the statement of item $(4)$ with $\ell = 1$) and the parabolic shape of semi-infinite geodesics ending at the top line of the Airy line ensemble, there exists some random integer $Y > y$ such that $\pi[x\to (Y, 1)](y) \ge \ell + 1$. Now, by geodesic uniqueness in the Airy line ensemble, we have the ordering
\[
\pi[x_k\to (y, 1)](t)\le \pi[x_k\to (y, \ell)](t)\le \pi[x_k\to (Y, 1)](t)
\]
for all $k\ge \ell$ sufficiently large, $t\in [-\sqrt{k}/\sqrt{2x}, y]$. Taking $k\to \infty$, using the convergence of $\pi[x_k\to (y, 1)]$ and $\pi[x_k\to (Y, 1)]$ as $k\to \infty$ we see the jump times of $\pi[x_k\to (y, \ell)]$ remain bounded and thus have sub-sequential limits. We now show they have a unique limit. 

By \cite[Lemma 3.4]{sarkar2021brownian}, the geodesics $\pi[x\to (y, 1)]$ and $\pi[x\to (Y, 1)]$ eventually coalesce. Furthermore, again by Lemma 3.4 in \cite{sarkar2021brownian}, the jump times of $\pi[x\to (y, 1)]$ and $\pi[x\to (Y, 1)]$ eventually take distinct values and diverge to the left as the depth diverges. This means there exists some rational $T\le y$, $d(T)\ge \ell$ for which $(T, d(T))$ is on the zig-zags of both $\pi[x_k\to (y, 1)]$ and $\pi[x_k\to (Y, 1)]$ almost surely eventually in $k\ge 1$. Now, by geodesic uniqueness, one can obtain for all $k$ sufficiently large $\pi[x_k \to (y, \ell)]$ as a concatenation of $\pi[x_k\to (y, 1)]$ restricted to $[-\sqrt{k}/\sqrt{2x}, T]$ and the unique geodesic from $(T, d(T))$ to $(y, \ell)$. Thus, the limit of $\pi[x_k \to (y, \ell)]$ in the topology of jump times exists, which we call $\pi[x \to (y, \ell)]$ and is a semi-infinite geodesic with asymptotic direction $x> 0$. 

This establishes existence of semi-infinite geodesics ending at $(y, \ell)$, $y\in \ensuremath{\mathbb{R}}$, $\ell \ge 2$. We now show that the asymptotic shape in \eqref{def: semi-inf geo} and local geodesic property are sufficient to almost surely uniquely determine the above semi-infinite geodesics. Recall that item (1) with $\ell = 1$ has been established in Lemma 4.2 of \cite{dauvergne2022disjointoptimizersdirectedlandscape}. Now, suppose $\sigma$ is some semi-infinite geodesic with asymptotic direction $x> 0$ ending at $(y, \ell)$. Let $Y$ be a random integer as above. Then for all $0 < \varepsilon < x$ rational, by the asymptotic parabolic shape of $\sigma$ and geodesic uniqueness in the Airy line ensemble, one has that $\sigma$ will lie to the right of $\pi[x-\varepsilon \to (y, 1)]$ and to the left of $\pi[x+\varepsilon \to (Y, 1)]$. This ordering is then preserved taking the limit $\varepsilon \to 0$. By their aforementioned uniqueness, the semi infinite paths $\pi[x-\varepsilon \to (y, 1)]$ and $\pi[x+\varepsilon \to (Y, 1)]$ are monotone in $\varepsilon$. By the continuity of the Airy line ensemble and they thus converge to semi-infinite geodesics with asymptotic direction $x> 0$, being pointwise limits of $x$-directed semi-infinite geodesics. By Lemma 4.2 in \cite{dauvergne2022disjointoptimizersdirectedlandscape}, they converge almost surely to the semi-infinite geodesics $\pi[x \to (y, 1)]$ and $\pi[x \to (Y, 1)]$. Thus, due to their eventual coalescence, the path $\sigma$ also eventually agrees with $\pi[x \to (y, 1)]$ and $\pi[x \to (Y, 1)]$, which is then uniquely determined up to $y$ by geodesic uniqueness in the Airy line ensemble. This concludes the proof of item (1).

The same argument also proves the coalescence of semi-infinite geodesics with the same fixed asymptotic direction almost surely, item $(3)$ for $\ell \ge 2$. 

We now establish item (2), namely, the simultaneous existence for all $x\ge 0, y\in \ensuremath{\mathbb{R}}, \ell \ge 1$ of leftmost and rightmost geodesics with asymptotic directions $x$ ending at $(y, \ell)$ on an event of full probability. We will show that this is a deterministic fact on the event (of full probability) of the existence and uniqueness of semi-infinite geodesics with rational endpoints and asymptotic directions. We first consider $x> 0$. Indeed, we have from the previous discussion, we have almost surely for all $q\in \ensuremath{\mathbb{Q}}\cap (0, \infty), r\in\ensuremath{\mathbb{Q}}$ there exists a $q$-directed semi-infinite geodesic starting ending at $(r, \ell)$, $\pi[q\to (r, \ell)]$. Now to construction leftmost and rightmost geodesics for any $x> 0, y\in \ensuremath{\mathbb{R}}$, simply consider the monotone limits
 \[
     \pi^L[x\to (y, \ell)] = \sup_{\substack{n\in \ensuremath{\mathbb{N}}\,,\\\ensuremath{\mathbb{Q}}\ni z_n \nearrow x\,, \ensuremath{\mathbb{Q}}\ni y_n\nearrow y}}\pi[z_n, (y_n, \ell)]\text{ and } \pi^R[x\to (y_n, \ell)] = \inf_{\substack{n\in \ensuremath{\mathbb{N}}\,,\\\ensuremath{\mathbb{Q}}\ni w_n \searrow x\,, \ensuremath{\mathbb{Q}}\ni y_n\searrow y}}\pi[w_n, (y_n, \ell)]\,.
\]
The asymptotic parabolic shape can also be computed the limiting profiles of the approximating geodesics. Moreover, being limits of semi-infinite geodesics in the topology of jump times, the restrictions of limiting zig-zags on bounded intervals are geodesics by the continuity of the Airy line ensemble. The left-most/right-most properties are inherited by the uniqueness of geodesics with rational endpoints in the Airy line ensemble, which gives monotonicity of geodesics with rational endpoints. For a similar argument, see \cite[Lemma 3.5]{DOV} establishing the right left continuity of right/left most geodesics.

Now, to treat the case $x = 0$, note for any $y\in \ensuremath{\mathbb{R}}$ the path $\sigma \equiv \ell$ on $(-\infty, y]$ is locally a geodesic and has asymptotic shape $\lim_{t\to -\infty} \sigma(t) /(2t^2) = 0$. Thus, it is a $0$-directed semi-infinite geodesic. In particular, it is automatically the left-most such geodesic. Now, to construct the rightmost such geodesic, argue by approximation as above, taking the sequence of rational directions $w_n \searrow 0$.

To prove item $(4)$ when $\ell \ge 2$, it suffices to observe that for any fixed $c\in \ensuremath{\mathbb{R}}$, $\mathcal{A}(\cdot+c)\overset d = \mathcal{A}(\cdot) - 2ct - c^2$ on $\mathcal{C}^\ensuremath{\mathbb{N}}$, by the stationarity of $\mathcal{A}(\cdot) + (\cdot)^2$. This is because under this symmetry, translations of geodesics in the Airy line ensemble by a deterministic amount are also geodesics in the shifted Airy line ensemble.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- lemma-12: body_md -->
\label{lemma: geodesic approx}
    Almost surely, for any $x\ge 0$, $y\in \ensuremath{\mathbb{R}}$, $\ell\ge 1$ the left-most and right most semi-infinite geodesics $\pi^L[x\to (y, \ell)], \pi^R[x\to (y, \ell)]$ from $x$ to $(y, \ell)$ can be expressed as the pointwise limits
    \[
     \pi^L[x\to (y, \ell)] = \sup_{\substack{n\in \ensuremath{\mathbb{N}}\,,\\\ensuremath{\mathbb{Q}}\ni z_n\nearrow x, \ensuremath{\mathbb{Q}}\ni y_n \nearrow y}}\pi[z_n, (y_n, \ell)]\,,\qquad x> 0\,,
     \]
     and $\pi^L[0\to (y, \ell)]\equiv 0$. Moreover,
     \[
     \pi^R[x\to (y, \ell)] = \inf_{\substack{n\in \ensuremath{\mathbb{N}}\,,\\\ensuremath{\mathbb{Q}}\ni w_n \searrow x, \ensuremath{\mathbb{Q}}\ni y_n \searrow y}}\pi[w_n, (y_n, \ell)]\,.
    \]
    Moreover, almost surely, for all $-\infty < t \le y$, with $\square \in \{L, R\}$, the restrictions $\pi^\square[z_n, (y, \ell)]|_{[t, y]}$ and $\pi^\square[w_n, (y, \ell)]|_{[t, y]}$ agree with $\pi^L[x\to (y, \ell)]|_{[t, y]}$ and $\pi^R[x\to (y, \ell)]|_{[t, y]}$ for all $n$ sufficiently large (depending on $t$).
<!-- end lemma-12 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
The proof of the first part essentially follows from the ordering property of geodesics in the Airy line ensemble, see the proof of item $(1)$ in Lemma \ref{lemma: semi-inf geod properties}. 

 We now prove the second item. We show that on the event (of full probability, see the remark after Lemma \ref{lemma: geod location brown env lpp}, which we tacitly assume holds) of geodesic existence and uniqueness in the Airy line ensemble, this item is a deterministic fact.
 
In particular, we show that for all $x> 0$, $t\le y\in \ensuremath{\mathbb{R}}$, $\square \in \{L, R\}$ and rational approximating sequences $(z_n), (w_n)$ as in the statement, the restrictions $\pi^\square[z_n, (y, \ell)]|_{[t, y]}$ and $\pi^\square[w_n, (y, \ell)]|_{[t, y]}$ coincide with $\pi^L[x\to (y, \ell)]|_{[t, y]}$ and $\pi^R[x\to (y, \ell)]|_{[t, y]}$ for all $n$ sufficiently large (depending on $t$). Arguing as in the proof of item (2) of Lemma \ref{lemma: semi-inf geod properties}, the paths $\pi^\square[z_n, (y, \ell)]$ and $\pi^\square[w_n, (y, \ell)]$ converge in the topology of jump times to $\pi^L[x\to (y, \ell)]$ and $\pi^R[x\to (y, \ell)]$ respectively. The former paths will henceforth be referred to as \emph{approximating paths} and the latter the \emph{limiting paths}.
    
Indeed, as we had before, the pointwise convergence of geodesics implies convergence of jump times. Fix $x> 0$ and $y \in \ensuremath{\mathbb{R}}$, $\square\in \{L, R\}$. There are two cases to consider. 

Suppose the first jump of $\pi^\square[x\to (y, \ell)]$ is strictly less than the right endpoint $y$. Then as $n\to \infty$, the convergence of jump times and the uniqueness of geodesics in the Airy line ensemble will give that for any $t\le y$, for all $n$ sufficiently large, the approximating paths coincide with their limits on $[t, y]$. For otherwise, a `geodesic bubble' with rational endpoints between the approximating and limiting paths would form (see the illustration on the left of Figure \ref{fig: geod approx}).

Now, if the first or more jumps of the limiting paths occur at exactly $y$, we will still use a similar geodesic overlap argument, though this time on the approximating paths \emph{themselves}. If we are considering the rightmost path $\pi^R[x\to (y, \ell)]$, the argument is reduced to the previous case, as the jumps of the approximating paths are also forced to be $y$ if they are so for the limiting path. Now, in the left-most case, that is when the first or more jump times of the limiting left-most geodesic are exactly $y$, we claim eventually in $n$, the initial vertical segments of the approximating and limiting paths coincide. Indeed, if this was not the case, by the convergence of jump times of the approximating paths, a `geodesic bubble' between rational endpoints between two approximating paths would occur for sufficiently large $n$, depicted on the right of Figure \ref{fig: geod approx}, again violating geodesic uniqueness. Then, as for the rightmost case, one argues as before.

    \begin{figure}[ht]
        \centering
        \includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{Images/geod_approx_2.eps}
        \caption{Illustration of possible obstructions to the second part of the statement in Lemma \ref{lemma: geodesic approx}, depending on whether there is at least one jump at $y$ or not. From the moment there is \textbf{left}: overlap between two horizontal segments of geodesics $\pi^R[x\to (y, \ell)]$ and $\pi^\square[w_n\to (y, \ell)]$ (alternatively $\pi^L[x\to (y, \ell)]$ and $\pi^\square[z_n\to (y, \ell)]$) if there is no jump at $y$, or \textbf{right}: $\pi^\square[z_n\to (y, \ell)]$ and $\pi^\square[z_{n'}\to (y, \ell)]$ (with $n'> n \ge 1$, $n, n'$ sufficiently large) on the event at least one jump of $\pi^L[x\to (y, \ell)]$ occurs at $y$, a `geodesic bubble' between rational endpoints forms (here in {\color{pink} pink}), violating geodesic uniqueness.}
        \label{fig: geod approx}
    \end{figure}
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- definition-6: body_md -->
(Minkowski dimension)\label{def: Minkowski dimension}
Fix $n\in \ensuremath{\mathbb{N}}, r\ge 0$ and let $A$ be a bounded subset of $\mathbb{R}^n$, then $A$ is said to have \emph{(upper) Minkowski dimension $r$} if
\begin{align*}
 \displaystyle\limsup_{\varepsilon \rightarrow 0} \frac{\log N(\varepsilon)}{\log 1/\varepsilon} = r\,, 
\end{align*}
 where $N(\varepsilon)$ denotes the infimum of all cardinalities of $\varepsilon$-covers of $A$.
<!-- end definition-6 -->
<!-- lemma-13: body_md -->
\label{lemma: close-not-dj}
	Fix $y_1< y_2$ and let $K\subseteq (0, \infty)$ be bounded, bounded away from zero and countable with Minkowski dimension less than $r< 1/2$. Then for all $\varepsilon>0$,
	\begin{equation}\label{eq: close-not-dj}
	 \limsup_{n \to \infty} \ensuremath{\mathbb{P}} \bigg(\bigcup_{x\in K} \pi\{\overline{x-\varepsilon}\to \widehat{y_1}\}_n \;\;\; \mathrm{and} \;\;\; \pi\{\overline{x +\varepsilon}\to \widehat{y_2}\}_n \text{ are disjoint} \bigg) \le C\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}-r}\cdot \mathrm{e}^{d|\log\varepsilon|^\frac{5}{6}},
	\end{equation}
    for some constants $d, C>0$.
<!-- end lemma-13 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
By the remark in lemma \ref{lemma: geod location brown env lpp}, for all $n\in \ensuremath{\mathbb{N}}$, the geodesics $\pi\{\overline{x-\varepsilon}\to \widehat{y_1}\}$ and $\pi\{\overline{x+\varepsilon}\to \widehat{y_2}\}_n$ are up to a null event unique. Consider the function
    \[
    F^n_{x, \varepsilon}(u):= WB^n[(\overline{x+\varepsilon}, n)\to (\widehat{u}, 1)]- WB^n[(\overline{x-\varepsilon}, n)\to (\widehat{u}, 1)]\qquad u \in [y_1, y_2]\,.
    \]
    By the crossing lemma for geodesics in last passage percolation, Lemma \ref{lemma: crossing}, $F^n_{x, \varepsilon}$ is non-decreasing. Moreover, since the Brownian melons $WB^n$ are locally absolutely continuous with respect to independent Brownian motions (in fact the melons are equal in law to $n$-level Dyson Brownian motion and so satisfy the Brownian Gibbs property, see the remark after lemma \ref{lemma: geod location brown env lpp}), we have by Lemma \ref{lemma: crossing equiavlence}, almost surely for any fixed $u, v \in [y_1, y_2]$
    \begin{equation}\label{eq: melon monoto}
        F^n_{x, \varepsilon}(v) - F^n_{x, \varepsilon}(u) > 0\,, \text{ if and only if } \pi\{\overline{x-\varepsilon}\to \widehat{u}\}_n \;\;\; \mathrm{and} \;\;\; \pi\{\overline{x+\varepsilon}\to \widehat{v}\}_n \text{ are disjoint}\,.
    \end{equation}
    Now, consider the partition of $[y_1, y_2]$,
    \[
    a_0 = y_1, a_1 = \min\{y_1 + \varepsilon, y_2\}\,,\ldots a_k = \min\{y_1 + k\varepsilon, y_2\}\,,\ldots\,, a_N = \min\{ y_1 + N\varepsilon, y_2\}\,, N = \bigg\lceil \frac{y_2-y_1}{\varepsilon} \bigg\rceil\,.
    \]
    Define the events 
    \[
    E^{\varepsilon, x, u, v, n}:=\left\{\pi\{\overline{x-\varepsilon}\to \widehat{u}\}_n \;\;\; \mathrm{and} \;\;\; \pi\{\overline{x+\varepsilon}\to \widehat{v}\}_n \text{ are disjoint} \right\}\,, \quad x > 0\,, y_1 \le u < v \le y_1\,.
    \]
    Then by \eqref{eq: melon monoto} we have by the monotonicity of $F^n_{x, \varepsilon}$ (see Figure \ref{fig: geod config} for the case $N = 2$),
    \begin{align*}
        E^{\varepsilon, x, y_1, y_2, n} &= \{F^n_{x, \varepsilon}(y_2) - F^n_{x, \varepsilon}(y_1) > 0\} = \displaystyle\bigcup^{N-1}_{k = 0}\{F^n_{x, \varepsilon}(a_{k+1}) - F^n_{x, \varepsilon}(a_k) > 0\} = \displaystyle\bigcup^{N-1}_{k = 0}\{E^{\varepsilon, x, a_k, a_{k+1}, n}\}\,.
    \end{align*}
    By \cite[Lemma 4.5]{DOV}, we have up to null sets,for any fixed $y_1 \le u< v \le y_2$ the events $E^{\varepsilon, x, u, v, n}$ are included in the events 
    \begin{align*}
        E^{\varepsilon, x, u, u, n}_B :=\bigg\{\pi[(\overline{x-\varepsilon},n)\to (\widehat{u}, 1)]_n \;\mathrm{and} \; \pi[(\overline{x+\varepsilon},n)\to (\widehat{v}, 1)]_n \text{ are essentially disjoint}\bigg\}\,,
    \end{align*}
    where $B$ is a family of $n$-independent Brownian motions (suppressing the dependence on $n$) that is used in the pathwise construction of the $n$-melon $WB^n$, and $\pi[\cdot \to \cdot]_n$ the almost sure unique geodesics in this environment.
    
    By the invariance of Brownian motion under diffusive scaling and translation and \cite[Theorem 1.1]{Hammond_2019}, we have for all $1\le k \le N$,
\begin{align*}
    \limsup_{n\to \infty}\ensuremath{\mathbb{P}}(E^{\varepsilon, x, u, u, n}_B)\le C\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{2}}\cdot\mathrm{e}^{d|\log \varepsilon|^{\frac{5}{6}}}
\end{align*}
and some universal constants $C, d > 0$. We will henceforth enlarge or reduce universal constants $C, d>0$ as needed. 

Informally, the above uniform bounds exist because these events capture the disjointness of rescaled geodesics in Brownian last passage percolation, which, under the (KPZ) $1:2:3$ scaling are tight and converge to geodesics in the Airy sheet. Moreover, the decay in $\varepsilon$ can be interpreted as being due to the rareness of disjoint geodesics in the Airy sheet (over a fixed time horizon) with starting and ending points within $\varepsilon$ of each other.

This gives 
\[
\ensuremath{\mathbb{P}}(E^{\varepsilon, x, y_1, y_2, n})\le  C\lceil (y_2-y_1)/\varepsilon\rceil \cdot \varepsilon^{\frac{3}{2}}\cdot\mathrm{e}^{d|\log \varepsilon|^{\frac{5}{6}}}\le C\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\mathrm{e}^{d|\log \varepsilon|^{\frac{5}{6}}}\,.
\]

 Now, since $K$ is bounded, consider a family of $N(\varepsilon)$ neighbourhoods $[a_i, b_i]$ in $\ensuremath{\mathbb{R}}$ that cover $K$ where each neighbourhood has diameter at most $\varepsilon$. Then, we can estimate the above estimates and the monotonicity of geodesics
\begin{align*}
    &\limsup_{n\to \infty}\ensuremath{\mathbb{P}}\left(\displaystyle\bigcup_{x\in K} E^{\varepsilon, x, y_1, y_2, n}\right)\\
    &\le \limsup_{n\to \infty}\sum^{N(\varepsilon)}_{i=1}\ensuremath{\mathbb{P}}\left(\pi\{\overline{a_i-\varepsilon}\to \widehat{y_1}\}_n \;\;\; \mathrm{and} \;\;\; \pi\{\overline{b_i+\varepsilon}\to \widehat{y_2}\}_n \text{ are disjoint} \right)\\
    &\le C|N(\varepsilon)|\cdot (y_2-y_1) \cdot\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{e}^{d|\log(\varepsilon)|^{\frac{5}{6}}}\,,
\end{align*}
concluding the proof, noting that $K$ having Minkowski dimension $r<1/2$, the covering numbers satisfy $N(\varepsilon)\lesssim \varepsilon^{-r}$.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
Such a rarity of disjointness of geodesics can also be extended in some sense to the case where $K$ is compact and has Hausdorff dimension $r < 1/2$. This need not be an event a priori, being an uncountable union of events. However, it is contained in a proper event by geodesic monotonicity (which can be obtained using the almost sure uniqueness of geodesics in the Brownian melon environments with rational endpoints, see the remark after Lemma \ref{lemma: geod location brown env lpp}) whose probability we can explicitly control.
    
    This is because for any fixed $\beta > r$ less than one half, for all $\varepsilon> 0$, one has a family of covers $(U^\varepsilon_i)$ with $\operatorname{diam} (U_i)\le \varepsilon$ satisfying
    \[
    \lim_{\varepsilon \to 0} \sum_i \mathrm{diam} (U^\varepsilon_i)^\beta = 0.
    \]
    By compactness (Lebesgue's number lemma), there exists some $\eta_\varepsilon > 0$ such that for $x\in K, y > 0$ with $|x-y|\le 2\eta_\varepsilon$, there exists $U^\varepsilon_i$ such that $x, y \in U^\varepsilon_i$. By monotonicity of geodesics, this means we have the inclusion
    \[
    \displaystyle\bigcup_{x\in K} E^{\eta_\varepsilon, x, y_1, y_2, n}\subseteq \displaystyle \bigcup_i \{\pi\{\overline{\inf U^\varepsilon_i}\to \widehat{y_1}\}_n \;\;\; \mathrm{and} \;\;\; \pi\{\overline{\sup U^\varepsilon_i}\to \widehat{y_2}\}_n \text{ are disjoint}\}\,.
    \]
    
    We can thus estimate arguing as in the proof of Lemma \ref{lemma: close-not-dj},
    \begin{align*}
        \limsup_{n\to \infty}\ensuremath{\mathbb{P}}\left(\displaystyle\bigcup_{x\in K} E^{\eta_\varepsilon, x, y_1, y_2, n}\right) &\le C\sum_i \mathrm{diam} (U^\varepsilon_i)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{e}^{d|\log\mathrm{diam} (U^\varepsilon_i)|^{\frac{5}{6}}}\\
        &\le C \varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}-\beta}\mathrm{e}^{d|\log \varepsilon|^{\frac{5}{6}}} \sup_{\varepsilon> 0}\sum_i \mathrm{diam} (U^\varepsilon_i)^\beta \\
        &\le C_\beta\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}-\beta}\mathrm{e}^{d|\log\varepsilon|^{\frac{5}{6}}}\,.
    \end{align*}

We will see later that this condition on the Hausdorff dimension is sharp for semi-infinite geodesics in the Airy line ensemble (which arise as scaling limits of these melon paths, see the coupling \pageref{Skorokhod coupling} below). This is due to exceptional sets of asymptotic directions and endpoints for which geodesic non-uniqueness occurs, see Theorem \ref{thm: airy sheet monotone haus dim}.
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{thm: semi-inf tail disj proba}
    Fix $\eta > 0, y_1 < y_2\in \ensuremath{\mathbb{R}}$ and $x> 0$. Then, for all $\eta>0$, there exists $\varepsilon_0(\eta)$ so that for all $0< \varepsilon < \min\{x, \varepsilon_0(\eta)\}$,
    \begin{equation*}
	C \cdot\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+\eta}\le \ensuremath{\mathbb{P}} \bigg(\pi[x-\varepsilon \to (y_1, 1)] \;\;\; \mathrm{and} \;\;\; \pi[x+\varepsilon \to (y_2, 1)] \text{ are disjoint} \bigg) \le C'\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+o(1)}
	\end{equation*}
    for constants $C, C', d > 0$.
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- remark-4: body_md -->
Leveraging the coupling between the Airy line ensemble and the Airy sheet in \ref{coupling: Airy sheet}, using similar arguments one can show there exist some universal constants $c, d> 0$ such that for any $0< x_1< x_2$, $y_1, y_2\in \ensuremath{\mathbb{R}}$,
    \[
     \ensuremath{\mathbb{P}} \bigg(\pi[x_1 \to (y_1, 1)] \;\;\; \mathrm{and} \;\;\; \pi[x_2 \to (y_2, 1)] \text{ are not disjoint} \bigg) \le c\mathrm{e}^{-d(|x_1-x_2|\land |y_1-y_2|)^3}.
    \]
<!-- end remark-4 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
Fix $\eta > 0, y_1<y_2$, $x> 0$ and $0 < \varepsilon < x$. We work with the coupling on page \pageref{Skorokhod coupling} and restrict ourselves to the event of full probability of semi-infinite geodesic uniqueness (with rational endpoints and asymptotic directions), guaranteed by Lemma \ref{lemma: semi-inf geod properties}. On this event, we have the monotonicity of disjointness events with respect to the geodesic endpoints and asymptotic directions. Thus, by the approximation lemma, \ref{lemma: geodesic approx}, we can without loss of generality take all $x, y_1, y_2, \varepsilon$ to be rational. 
    
    Let 
     \[
    \mathsf{Disj}_{x,y_1, y_2, \varepsilon} := \{\pi[x-\varepsilon \to (y_1, 1)] \;\;\; \mathrm{and} \;\;\; \pi[x+\varepsilon \to (y_2, 1)] \text{ are disjoint}\}.
    \]

\underline{Upper bound}:
    We first show using the coupling in page \pageref{Skorokhod coupling} between Brownian melons and the Airy line ensemble, that if (recall the notation for geodesics in Brownian melon environments from the discussion before Lemma \ref{lemma: close-not-dj})
    \[
    \pi\{\overline{x-\varepsilon}\to \widehat{y_1}\}_n \;\;\; \mathrm{and} \;\;\; \pi\{\overline{x +\varepsilon}\to \widehat{y_2}\}_n  \text{ are not disjoint infinitely often in }n\,,
    \]
    the geodesics $\pi[x-\varepsilon \to (y_1, 1)]$ and $\pi[x+\varepsilon \to (y_2, 1)]$ are not disjoint. This will then allow us to use the estimates from Lemma \ref{lemma: close-not-dj}. A defect of the coupling is that the statements holds for some subsequence of Brownian melons. We can harmlessly identify this with $\ensuremath{\mathbb{N}}$ and not do any further relabelling.

    By Property 2. of the coupling on page \pageref{Skorokhod coupling}, there exists a random $N\in \ensuremath{\mathbb{N}}$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq: geod parabol monoton 2}
    Z_{k}(x-\varepsilon, y_1) < Z_k(x, y_1)\,,
\end{equation}
for all $k\ge N$. This means the semi-infinite geodesics $\pi[x-\varepsilon \to (y_1, 1)]$ and $\pi[x \to (y_1, 1)]$ on $\mathcal{A}$ branch on or before level $N$, consistent with their asymptotic parabolic shapes. By Property 2. of the coupling \pageref{Skorokhod coupling}, \eqref{eq: geod parabol monoton 2}, we have the almost sure convergence of jump times
\[
Z^n_{\ell}(x-\varepsilon, y_1)\stackrel{n\to \infty}{\longrightarrow}Z_{\ell}(x-\varepsilon, y_1)\,,\quad Z^n_{\ell}(x+\varepsilon, y_2)\stackrel{n\to \infty}{\longrightarrow}Z_{\ell}(x+\varepsilon, y_2)\,,\qquad 1\le \ell \le k\,.
\]
Thus, on the event $\{N = k\}$, \eqref{eq: geod parabol monoton 2} means that for all $n$ sufficiently large, $Z^n_{k}(x-\varepsilon, y_1) < Z^n_{k}(x, y)$. Consequently, by the tree-like nature of geodesics, Lemma \ref{lemma: geod tree}, the geodesics $\pi\{\overline{x-\varepsilon}\to \widehat{y_1}\}_n$ and $\pi\{\overline{x}\to \widehat{y_1}\}_n$ branch before or at depth $k$ (since otherwise this would violate geodesic uniqueness on the melons $WB^n$, which we tacitly assume throughout).

Therefore, on the event 
\[
    \pi\{\overline{x-\varepsilon}\to \widehat{y_1}\}_n \;\;\; \mathrm{and} \;\;\; \pi\{\overline{x +\varepsilon}\to \widehat{y_2}\}_n  \text{ are not disjoint infinitely often in }n\,,
\]
we have by geodesic monotonicity that for all $n$ sufficiently large, the zig-zags of geodesics $\pi\{\overline{x-\varepsilon}\to \widehat{y_1}\}_n$ and $\pi\{\overline{x+\varepsilon}\to \widehat{y_2}\}_n$ cannot meet past level $k$, for this would create a `geodesic bubble', violating the tree-structure of the geodesics  $\pi\{\overline{x-\varepsilon}\to \widehat{y_1}\}_n$ and $\pi\{\overline{x}\to \widehat{y_1}\}_n$. Since this $k$ is uniform in $n\ge 1$, taking $n\to \infty$ and using the convergence of jump times, we obtain by compactness that the zig-zags of $\pi[x-\varepsilon \to (y_1, 1)]$ and $\pi[x+\varepsilon \to (y_2, 1)]$ share a common point. Since $k\ge 1$ was arbitrary, we conclude the event 
\[
\pi[x-\varepsilon \to (y_1, 1)] \;\;\; \mathrm{and} \;\;\; \pi[x+\varepsilon \to (y_2, 1)] \text{ are disjoint} 
\]
is contained in the event
\[
    \pi\{\overline{x-\varepsilon}\to \widehat{y_1}\}_n \;\;\; \mathrm{and} \;\;\; \pi\{\overline{x +\varepsilon}\to \widehat{y_2}\}_n  \text{ are disjoint eventually in }n\,.
\]
Thus, Fatou's lemma and the bounds in Lemma \ref{lemma: close-not-dj} give the desired result.
    
\underline{Lower bound}:
We proceed by way of contradiction. Suppose that there exists some $\eta > 0$ such that
\[
\liminf_{\varepsilon > 0}\frac{\ensuremath{\mathbb{P}} \bigg(\pi[x-\varepsilon \to (y_1, 1)] \;\;\; \mathrm{and} \;\;\; \pi[x+\varepsilon \to (y_2, 1)] \text{ are disjoint} \bigg)}{\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+\eta}} < \infty\,.
\]

We claim that by the planarity of geodesics, Lemma \ref{lemma: crossing equiavlence} and the eventual coalescence of semi-infinite geodesics in the Airy line ensemble, items $(1), (2), (3)$ in Lemma \ref{lemma: semi-inf geod properties}, we obtain (up to a null set)
\[
\pi[x-\varepsilon \to (y_1, 1)] \;\;\; \mathrm{and} \;\;\; \pi[x+\varepsilon \to (y_2, 1)] \text{ are disjoint if and only if } \Delta^{(y_1, 1)}_{(y_2, 1)}(x+\varepsilon) > \Delta^{(y_1, 1)}_{(y_2, 1)}(x-\varepsilon)
\]
eventually in $\varepsilon \to 0$ (rational).

Indeed, using the uniqueness and coalescing properties of semi-infinite geodesics with the same \emph{fixed} asymptotic direction $x> 0$, items $(1), (3)$ in Lemma \ref{lemma: semi-inf geod properties} and \cite[Lemma 3.4]{sarkar2021brownian} we have for any rational $0<\varepsilon < x$, $\pi[x-\varepsilon \to (y_1, 1)]$ and $\pi[x+\varepsilon\to (y_2, 1)]$ are pointwise limits of the geodesics $\pi[(x-\varepsilon)_k \to (y_1, 1)]$ and $\pi[(x+\varepsilon)_k \to (y_2, 1)]$ (recall the notation from Section \ref{sec: semi-inf geod}, $x_k := -\sqrt{k}/\sqrt{2x}$, $x> 0$, $k\ge 1$). Moreover, the convergence of jump times implies for a fixed, possible random rational $t\le y_1$, the paths $\pi[(x-\varepsilon)_k \to (y_1, 1)]$ and $\pi[(x+\varepsilon)_k \to (y_2, 1)]$ almost surely eventually stabilise to their limits on $[t, y_1]$ (see the illustration in Figure \ref{fig: geod approx}). Moreover, by the distinct asymptotic shapes of $\pi[x-\varepsilon \to (y_1, 1)]$ and $\pi[x+\varepsilon \to (y_2, 1)]$ (see \eqref{def: semi-inf geo}), there exists some rational $T < 0$ such that on $(-\infty, T]$, the zig-zags of $\pi[x-\varepsilon \to (y_1, 1)]$ and $\pi[x+\varepsilon \to (y_2, 1)]$ are disjoint. We thus obtain for all $k\ge 1$ by a direct application of Lemma \ref{lemma: crossing equiavlence},
 \[
\pi[(x-\varepsilon)_k \to (y_1, 1)] \;\;\; \mathrm{and} \;\;\; \pi[(x+\varepsilon)_k\to (y_2, 1)]\]
are disjoint if and only if
\[
\mathcal{A}[(x+\varepsilon)_k \to (y_2, 1)]-\mathcal{A}[(x+\varepsilon)_k \to (y_1, 1)] > \mathcal{A}[(x-\varepsilon)_k \to (y_2, 1)]-\mathcal{A}[(x-\varepsilon)_k \to (y_1, 1)].
\]
Thus, taking $k\to \infty$, we obtain the promised equivalence. 

By the coupling between the Airy sheet and the Airy line ensemble, see \ref{coupling: Airy sheet}, we have almost surely,
\[
\mathcal{S}(x, y_2)-\mathcal{S}(x, y_1) = \Delta^{(y_1, 1)}_{(y_2, 1)}(x)\,,\quad x\in (0, \infty)\cap \ensuremath{\mathbb{Q}}.
\] 
The quadrangle inequality satisfied by the Airy sheet, \eqref{eq: quadrangle ineq airy sheet} (entirely analogous to the crossing lemma, Lemma \ref{lemma: crossing} for geodesics in last passage percolation), yields that the disjointness of the geodesics $\pi[x-\varepsilon \to (y_1, 1)]$ and $\pi[x+\varepsilon \to (y_2, 1)]$ is equivalent to the `Airy difference profile' $x\mapsto \mathcal{S}(x, y_2)-\mathcal{S}(x, y_1)$ being non-constant on $[x-\varepsilon, x+ \varepsilon]$. Hence, we have for the $x$ above,
\begin{equation}\label{eq: lv airy diff proba bound}
    \liminf_{\varepsilon > 0}\frac{\ensuremath{\mathbb{P}} \bigg(\mathsf{LV}(\mathcal{S}(x, y_2)-\mathcal{S}(x, y_1), [x-\varepsilon, x+\varepsilon])\neq \emptyset \bigg)}{\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+\eta}} < \infty\,,
\end{equation}
where for $a< b$ and a function $f:[a, b] \to \ensuremath{\mathbb{R}}$, we denote the \emph{set of local variation}, 
\[
\mathsf{LV}(f, [a, b]) = \left\{\begin{aligned}
    &c\in [a, b]: f \text{ is non-constant there on every}\\
    &\text{neighbourhood of }c \text{ in } [a, b]
\end{aligned}\right\}\,.
\]
By translation symmetries \eqref{eq: Airy sheet symmetry} of the Airy sheet $\mathcal{S}$, for any $c \in \ensuremath{\mathbb{R}}$, the process $x\mapsto \mathcal{S}(x, y_2)-\mathcal{S}(x, y_1)$ has stationary increments. This means the above estimates hold for all $x\in \ensuremath{\mathbb{R}}$.

Now, for $a < b$ finite, consider the interval $[a, b]$. For any $\varepsilon > 0$ (here we will take the deterministic subsequence from \eqref{eq: lv airy diff proba bound} which we will not relabel), starting from $a$, partition $[a, b]$ into a family of intervals of diameter $2\varepsilon$, possibly overshooting $b$ in the last step. Let $N_\varepsilon\le \lceil(b-a)/(2\varepsilon)\rceil$ be the random number of intervals above for which the (non-decreasing by \eqref{eq: quadrangle ineq airy sheet}) difference profile $x\mapsto \mathcal{S}(x, y_2)-\mathcal{S}(x, y_1)$ experiences an increase. Using linearity and taking expectations, we have the estimate
\[
\mathbb{E}[N_\varepsilon] \le \bigg\lceil\frac{b-a}{2\varepsilon}\bigg\rceil\cdot\ensuremath{\mathbb{P}} \bigg(\mathsf{LV}(\mathcal{S}(a, y_2)-\mathcal{S}(a, y_1), [a, a+2\varepsilon])\neq \emptyset \bigg)\le C_{a, b}\cdot\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}+\eta}
\]
for some constant $C_{a, b} > 0$.

We can thus cover the set of local variation $\mathsf{LV}(\mathcal{S}(\cdot, y_2)-\mathcal{S}(\cdot, y_1), [a, b])$ by $N_\varepsilon$ many intervals $(U^i_\varepsilon)_i$ of diameter $2\varepsilon$ such that
\[
\sum_i \operatorname{diam}(U^i_\varepsilon)^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\eta}{2}}\le C_{a, b, \eta}  \cdot N_\varepsilon\cdot \varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\eta}{2}}
\]
for some constant $C_{a, b, \eta} > 0$. Taking expectations, we have
\[
 \mathbb{E}[\sum_i \operatorname{diam}(U^i_\varepsilon)^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\eta}{2}}]\le C_{a, b, \eta}\cdot \varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}+\eta} \cdot \varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\eta}{2}} \le C_{a, b, \eta} \cdot \varepsilon^{\frac{\eta}{2}}\,, 
\]
which converges to zero as $\varepsilon \to 0$. Thus, (after possibly passing to a further subsequence of $\varepsilon \searrow 0$) almost surely, the Hausdorff dimension of $\mathsf{LV}(\mathcal{S}(\cdot, y_2)-\mathcal{S}(\cdot, y_1), [a, b])$ is at most $1/2 - \eta /2 < 1/2$. Since $a< b$ were arbitrary, we obtain that the Hausdorff dimension of  $\mathsf{LV}(\mathcal{S}(\cdot, y_2)-\mathcal{S}(\cdot, y_1), \ensuremath{\mathbb{R}})$ is at most $1/2 - \eta /2 < 1/2$. This is a contradiction as it is almost surely one half \cite[Theorem 1.1]{Basu2021Fractal}.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{prop: Busemann regularity}
  Almost surely, for any fixed $x\in \ensuremath{\mathbb{Q}}\cap(0, \infty)$, $m, m' \in \ensuremath{\mathbb{N}}$, the map\\ \noindent $(t, t')\mapsto \Delta^{(t, m)}_{(t', m')}(x)$ has a modification in $\mathcal{C}(\ensuremath{\mathbb{R}}^2)$. Moreover, the process $\ensuremath{\mathbb{Q}}\cap(0, \infty)\ni x\mapsto \big(t, m, t', m'\mapsto \Delta^{(t, m)}_{(t', m')}(x)\big)$ has right and left limits, with convergence uniform in $(t, t', m, m')$ on bounded subsets of $\ensuremath{\mathbb{Q}}^2\times \ensuremath{\mathbb{N}}$.
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
For fixed $x > 0$ rational, the local uniform continuity of the map $(t, m, t', m')\mapsto \Delta^{(t, m)}_{(t', m')}(x)$ on $(\ensuremath{\mathbb{Q}}\times \ensuremath{\mathbb{N}})^2$ follows from the uniform coalescence of geodesics with fixed asymptotic direction and bounded right endpoints, item $(3)$ of Lemma \ref{lemma: semi-inf geod properties}. In particular, for any bounded subset $K\subset (\ensuremath{\mathbb{Q}}\times \ensuremath{\mathbb{N}})^2$, all geodesics $\pi[x\to (t, m)]$ and $\pi[x\to (t', m')]$, for $(t, m , t', m ')\in K$ pass through a common point and coalesce thereafter. This means the Busemann limit in \eqref{eq: Airy limit busemann} almost surely eventually stabilises uniformly in $k\ge1$ for $(t, m , t', m ')\in K$ in any fixed bounded set, giving $\Delta^{(t, m)}_{(t', m')}(x)$ as a difference of two finite depth last passage values. 
    
    Now to establish the second part, we show that for any monotone sequence $(x_n)\subseteq \ensuremath{\mathbb{Q}}\cap(0, \infty)$ converging to some $x > 0$, on any bounded subset of $(\ensuremath{\mathbb{Q}}\times \ensuremath{\mathbb{N}})^2$, the uniform norms $\left\lVert\Delta^{\cdot}_{\cdot}(x_n)-\Delta^{\cdot}_{\cdot}(x_m)\right\rVert$ converge to zero as $m, n \to \infty$ by the monotonicity of the Busemann profiles and the planarity of geodesics, Lemma \ref{lemma: crossing}. Indeed, two application of Lemma \ref{lemma: crossing} gives for all $s\le t, t'\le r$ rational, $n\le m, m'\le N$ and $0< x< x'$ rational,
    \begin{align*}
        0&\le \sup_{\substack{s\le t, t'\le r\\n\le m ,m'\le N}}|\Delta^{(t, m)}_{(t', m')}(x')- \Delta^{(t, m)}_{(t', m')}(x)| \le \Delta^{(s, n)}_{(r, N)}(x')- \Delta^{(s, n)}_{(r, N)}(x)\to 0\,, \quad x'\to x\,,
    \end{align*}
    where the first inequality follows from Lemma \ref{lemma: crossing} applied to the approximating paths in Lemma \ref{lemma: semi-inf geod properties}, and the convergence to zero comes from the monotonicity of the Busemann processes $\Delta^{(s, n)}_{(r, N)}(\cdot)$.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{prop: non-disj-close-endpts-inf-geo}
    Fix $\ell \ge 2$. Then, for any fixed rational $0<a< b$,
    \[
    \mathsf{LV}\big(\Delta^{(0,1)}_{(0,\ell)}(u)\,, u\in [a, b]\big)\neq\emptyset \text{ if and only if } \pi[a \to (0, 1)] \;\;\; \mathrm{and} \;\;\; \pi[b \to  (0, \ell)] \text{ are disjoint}\,.
    \]
    
    Moreover, for all rational $0< \varepsilon < x$,
    \begin{equation}\label{eq: LV estimate}
	\ensuremath{\mathbb{P}}\bigg(\mathsf{LV}\big(\Delta^{(0,1)}_{(0,\ell)}(u)\,, u\in [x-\varepsilon, x+\varepsilon]\big)\neq\emptyset\bigg)\le C_x \cdot \varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}+o_\varepsilon(1)}
	\end{equation}
    for some constant $C_x > 0$ that is uniformly bounded when $x$ is on a bounded subset of $(0, \infty)$, bounded away from zero.
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- remark-5: body_md -->
Observe that by the stationarity of semi-infinite geodesics with respect to horizontal shifts, item $(3)$ of Lemma \ref{lemma: semi-inf geod properties}, the same results hold verbatim for all Busemann function $\Delta^{(y,1)}_{(y,\ell)}$ with $y\in \ensuremath{\mathbb{R}}$.
<!-- end remark-5 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
Fix rational $0 < x< z$, and $\ell \ge 2$. We claim that
\[
    \pi[x \to (0, 1)] \;\;\; \mathrm{and} \;\;\; \pi[z \to  (0, \ell)] \text{ are disjoint if and only if } \Delta_{(0, \ell)}^{(0, 1)}(z) >  \Delta_{(0, \ell)}^{(0, 1)}(x)\,.
    \]
    We will proceed as in the proof of Theorem \ref{thm: semi-inf tail disj proba}, where this was established for $\ell = 1$.
    
    More precisely, using the uniqueness and coalescing properties of semi-infinite geodesics with the same \emph{fixed} asymptotic direction $x> 0$, items $(1), (3)$ in Lemma \ref{lemma: semi-inf geod properties} and \cite[Lemma 3.4]{sarkar2021brownian} we have $\pi[x \to (0, 1)]$ and $\pi[z\to (0, \ell)]$ are pointwise limits of the geodesics $\pi[x_k \to (0, 1)]$ and $\pi[z_k\to (0, \ell)]$ (recall the definition from Section \ref{sec: semi-inf geod}). By the distinct asymptotic shapes of $\pi[x \to (0, 1)]$ and $\pi[z\to (0, \ell)]$ (see \eqref{def: semi-inf geo}), there exists some rational $T < 0$ such that on $(-\infty, T]$, the zig-zags of $\pi[x \to (0, 1)]$ and $\pi[z\to (0, \ell)]$ are disjoint. Arguing as in the approximation lemma, Lemma \ref{lemma: geodesic approx}, the convergence of jump times implies for a fixed, possible random rational $t\le 0$, the paths $\pi[x_k \to (0, 1)]$ and $\pi[z_k \to (0, \ell)]$ almost surely eventually stabilise to their limits on $[t, 0]$ (see the illustration in Figure \ref{fig: geod approx}) Thus, the pointwise convergence of jump times and the approximation lemma, \ref{lemma: geodesic approx} implies the disjointness of $\pi[x \to (0, 1)]$ and $\pi[z \to (0, \ell)]$ is equivalent to the almost sure eventual disjointness of $\pi[x_k \to (0, 1)]$ and $\pi[z_k \to (0, \ell)]$ on $[T, 0]$ as $k\to \infty$.
    
    We also obtain by a direct application of Lemma \ref{lemma: crossing equiavlence}, that almost surely for all $k \ge \ell$
    \[
    \pi[x_k \to (0, 1)] \;\;\; \mathrm{and} \;\;\; \pi[z_k\to (0, \ell)]
    \]
    are disjoint (recall from Definition \ref{def: disjointness}) if and only if
     \[
    \mathcal{A}[z_k \to (0, \ell)]-\mathcal{A}[z_k \to (0, 1)] > \mathcal{A}[x_k \to (0, \ell)]-\mathcal{A}[x_k \to (0, 1)]\,.
    \]
    By item (3) in Lemma \ref{lemma: semi-inf geod properties} the above differences of last passage values eventually stabilise in $k\ge \ell$. 
    
    Hence, taking $k\to \infty$, using the definition of the Busemann processes in \eqref{eq: Airy limit busemann}, we conclude the proof of the claim. 
 
Now, fix $0 < \varepsilon < \min\{x, 1\}$, $y> 0$. On the event the jump time $Z_\ell(x+\varepsilon, y)$ of geodesic $\pi[x+\varepsilon, (y, 1)]$ from level $\ell$ to $\ell-1$ is greater than zero, if the geodesics $\pi[x-\varepsilon\to (0, 1)]$ and $\pi[x+\varepsilon \to (y, 1)]$ are not disjoint, then by monotonicity geodesics $\pi[x-\varepsilon\to (0, 1)]$ and $\pi[x+\varepsilon \to (0, \ell)]$ are also not disjoint. Thus, a union bound on the complement of the intersection of the above events gives
\begin{align*}
    &\ensuremath{\mathbb{P}}\bigg(\mathsf{LV}\big(\Delta^{(0,1)}_{(0,\ell)}(u)\,, u\in (x-\varepsilon, x+\varepsilon)\big)\neq\emptyset\bigg)\\
    &= \ensuremath{\mathbb{P}}\bigg(\pi[x-\varepsilon \to (0, 1)] \;\;\; \mathrm{and} \;\;\; \pi[x+\varepsilon \to  (0, \ell)] \text{ are disjoint} \bigg)\\
    &\le \ensuremath{\mathbb{P}}\left( \pi[x-\varepsilon \to (0, 1)] \;\;\; \mathrm{and} \;\;\; \pi[x+\varepsilon  \to  (y, 1)] \text{ are disjoint} \right) + \ensuremath{\mathbb{P}}\bigg(Z_{\ell}(x+\varepsilon, y)\le 0\bigg)\\
    &\le  \ensuremath{\mathbb{P}}\left( \pi[x-\varepsilon \to (0, 1)] \;\;\; \mathrm{and} \;\;\; \pi[x+\varepsilon  \to  (y, 1)] \text{ are disjoint} \right)\\
    &+ \ensuremath{\mathbb{P}}\bigg(Z_{\ell}(x+\varepsilon, 0)+y\le 0\bigg)\,,
\end{align*}
where the last line follows by item$(4)$ in Lemma \ref{lemma: semi-inf geod properties}, which gives the equality in law $Z_\ell(x+\varepsilon, y) \overset d = Z_\ell(x+\varepsilon , 0) + y$. We now estimate the lower tails of the geodesic jump times $Z_\ell (x+\varepsilon, 0)$. 

Fix $z> 0$, by geodesic coalescence, \cite[Lemma 3.4]{sarkar2021brownian}, we have there exists some random $T <0$ rational and depth $d(T) \in \ensuremath{\mathbb{N}}$ such that all the geodesics $\pi[z_k \to (-y, 1)]$ and $\pi[z_k\to (0, 1)]$ almost surely eventually coalesce as $k\to \infty$.

By the coupling between the Airy sheet and the Airy line ensemble, \ref{coupling: Airy sheet}, we have 
\[
\mathcal{S}(z,0)-\mathcal{S}(z,-y) = \mathcal{A}[(T, d(T))\to (0, 1)]- \mathcal{A}[(T, d(T))\to (-y, 1)]\,.
\]
Since last passage values become additive on the graphs of their geodesics (by the metric composition law, see \ref{lemma: Metric Composition}), on the event $\{Z_\ell(x, 0) \le -y\}$, we have the upper bounds
\begin{equation}\label{eq:comparison}
 \mathcal{S}(z,0)-\mathcal{S}(z,-y)
 \le \mathcal{A}[(-y, \ell)\to (0,1)] \le \mathcal{A}_1(0)-\mathcal{A}_\ell(-y)
 \quad\text{on }\{Z_\ell(x,0)\le-y\}\,,
\end{equation}
where the latter inequality comes from the monotonicity of the Airy lines, and the definition of the length functional, see Definition \ref{def: length}.
Rearranging \eqref{eq:comparison} shows that with $r = zy$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cost}
 \{Z_\ell(x,0)\le-y\}
 \subseteq
